# Wander Bike — British Columbia Cycling Guide Master File

_Research pass: 8 August 2026_

> **Purpose:** a province-wide working file for Wander Bike. It keeps every City, Town, Village, District and special local-government candidate in the research pool so the site owner—not the research process—decides what to publish.

## What is inside

- **161 destination/jurisdiction candidates** in one master index, including Cities, Towns, Villages, Districts and special municipal-government classifications.
- **77,000+ words of English publish-ready material** across the master file, with a deeper Richmond flagship guide.
- SEO title, meta description and suggested URL for every destination.
- Destination-specific or regional ride anchors, practical planning copy, source IDs and research-strength labels.
- A current-condition watchlist for route issues that can make old blog posts misleading.
- 70 official, municipal, regional, park, trail-management and destination sources in the source desk.

## How to use this file

- The prose under each destination is written as **publish-ready English website copy**.
- Editorial source notes are stored inside `<!-- HTML comments -->`. Most Markdown/CMS renderers hide these from visitors, but always preview your CMS before publishing.
- **A+/A/B/C/HOLD are research-depth labels, not a recommendation to delete a place.** A+ and A have the strongest destination-specific material; B has good regional support; C needs more local verification for exact routes; HOLD means an access/cultural verification gate is required.
- Distances and opening/closure notes are only as current as the **8 August 2026** research pass. Recheck official sources before publishing or refreshing a page.
- Never imply first-hand experience if Wander Bike has not personally ridden the route. The copy deliberately avoids claims such as “we rode this.”

## Province-wide reusable cycling note

British Columbia rewards careful route planning because the province ranges from flat river dykes and paved urban greenways to gravel rail trails, steep mountain networks and remote roads with long distances between services. Choose a route for the bike and rider you actually have—not just the distance shown on a map.

B.C. cyclists must follow traffic controls and applicable road rules, use required lighting and reflectors after dark, and wear an approved bicycle helmet. Local bylaws, parks and trail managers may add restrictions, including rules for e-bikes or shared pathways. For ferry-linked rides, BC Ferries publishes current bicycle boarding information. Always check the operator, municipality, park or trail manager immediately before the ride.

<!-- Core reusable sources: S02 provincial bike/e-bike rules; S03 BC Ferries bicycle information. -->

## 2026 Route-status watchlist

These items are current enough to affect what Wander Bike should publish now. They should be rechecked whenever the related page is updated.

- **Richmond — North Dyke Trail:** the City of Richmond currently shows a closure between roughly 1 km east of No. 5 Road and No. 6 Road, with a detour using No. 5 Road, Vulcan Way and No. 6 Road. Do not publish a “continuous North Dyke” itinerary without showing the detour.
- **Richmond — George Massey Tunnel:** bicycles cannot be ridden through the Highway 99 / George Massey Tunnel. Riders need transit or the provincial bicycle shuttle when it is operating.
- **Kelowna / Lake Country — Okanagan Rail Trail:** a gap remains north of Kelowna International Airport. Construction on a 3.2 km section began in March 2026, while the remaining connection depends on a separate approval process. Do not describe the full Vernon–Kelowna corridor as continuously rideable yet.
- **Sicamous / Enderby / Armstrong — Shuswap North Okanagan Rail Trail:** as of the July 2026 update, roughly 32 km are open in disconnected sections. Grindrod–Enderby remains closed, and temporary work near Mara Lake is also planned. Use the current project map rather than old “complete route” descriptions.
- **Victoria / Sooke / West Shore — Galloping Goose:** CRD construction and maintenance alerts can affect individual sections. Check the current Galloping Goose page before publishing exact through-route instructions.
- **Penticton / South Okanagan — Kettle Valley Rail Trail:** conditions and access vary by segment, and current notices include closures or motorized-use sections. Avoid treating every KVR segment as identical or traffic-free.
- **Ucluelet — Wild Pacific Trail:** the Wild Pacific Trail is a walking trail and bicycles are not allowed. It can be mentioned as an off-bike attraction, but never as a cycling route.
- **Pacific Rim / Tofino:** Parks Canada permits cycling on designated areas including the paved ʔapsčiik t̓ašii pathway; do not assume every hiking trail in Pacific Rim National Park Reserve allows bicycles.

# Richmond Cycling Guide Hub — Flagship publish-ready copy

**Suggested hub URL:** `/guides/richmond-bc-cycling-guide`  
**SEO title:** `Cycling in Richmond, BC: 8 Scenic Bike Routes & Local Guide | Wander Bike`  
**Meta description:** `Explore Richmond by bike with 8 city-published scenic loops, Steveston and dyke routes, Sea Island rides, distance estimates, surfaces and current route notes.`

## Cycling in Richmond, BC

Richmond is one of the easiest places in Metro Vancouver to turn a bicycle into a full day of sightseeing. The city is mostly flat, the Fraser River wraps around the islands, and a large network of dykes, greenways and local bikeways links Steveston, Terra Nova, Middle Arm, Sea Island and East Richmond. For visitors who would rather enjoy the scenery than climb hills, Richmond is unusually forgiving.

The City of Richmond reports more than 345 km of designated on-street and off-street cycling routes. It also publishes eight recreational cycling loops, ranging from a short 6 km Steveston and South Dyke ride to a 47.5 km loop around Richmond Island. That makes the city useful for almost every kind of casual rider: families looking for a short waterfront outing, travellers who want a half-day ride, and stronger riders who want to circle a large part of the island.

The biggest mistake is to think of Richmond as one single “dyke trail.” The character changes from section to section. West Dyke is open and exposed, with broad sky and river views. South Dyke brings you closer to Steveston and the south arm of the Fraser. Middle Arm gives you a more urban waterfront ride with Canada Line access nearby. Sea Island adds airport-area paths and another perspective on the river. East Richmond is quieter and more rural in feel.

### Which Richmond ride should you choose?

Choose **Steveston / South Dyke** if you want the shortest visitor-friendly ride. Choose **West Dyke / South Dyke** for the classic Richmond experience. Choose **Sea Island / Middle Arm** if you want to combine cycling with Canada Line access. Choose one of the 38–43.5 km loops if you want a proper half-day outing, and choose the **47.5 km Circumnavigate the Island** route if distance is part of the fun.

## 8 official Richmond scenic cycling loops

The distances and approximate ride times below come from the City of Richmond's recreational cycling map. Times are estimates rather than promises; photo stops, wind, surface conditions and food breaks can change the day substantially.

### 1. Steveston / South Dyke — 6 km

**City estimate:** about 20 minutes at an 18 km/h riding pace.

This is the easiest introduction to cycling in Richmond. It works well for visitors who want to combine a short ride with Steveston Village, the waterfront and an unhurried meal. The route is also easy to extend: if everyone is comfortable after the first few kilometres, continue farther along South Dyke instead of ending the ride immediately.

**Best for:** first-time visitors, families, casual riders and short rentals.

### 2. West Dyke / South Dyke — 26.5 km

**City estimate:** about 90 minutes.

This is one of the best all-around Richmond rides because it combines two of the city's signature waterfront corridors. West Dyke gives you long open views and a relaxed, almost rural feeling even though you are still in Metro Vancouver. South Dyke then brings the ride toward Steveston and the Fraser River's south arm.

**Best for:** a classic Richmond half-day ride, scenery, visitors who want “the dyke experience.”

### 3. East Richmond South / North Arm River — 23.5 km

**City estimate:** about 75 minutes.

This loop shows a side of Richmond that many tourists miss. Instead of staying around Steveston and the west side, it uses the river landscape and quieter eastern parts of the city. It is a useful second ride for someone who has already done West Dyke.

**Best for:** repeat visitors, quieter riding and seeing more than Steveston.

### 4. Sea Island Drive / Middle Arm — 25 km

**City estimate:** about 85 minutes.

Sea Island and Middle Arm make a good combination for riders who like transportation infrastructure, river views and easy connections to rapid transit. Sea Island has its own cycling network around the airport area, while Middle Arm provides a waterfront spine on the Richmond side.

**Best for:** transit-assisted rides, aviation interest and a different Richmond waterfront.

### 5. West and South Dyke / Sea Island — 30 km

**City estimate:** about 1 hour 45 minutes.

This loop links several of Richmond's most recognizable riding environments into one moderate-distance outing. It is a good step up from a single dyke ride without committing to a full island circuit.

**Best for:** confident casual riders and a broad Richmond sampler.

### 6. Middle Arm / East & Central Richmond — 38 km

**City estimate:** about 2 hours.

At 38 km, this starts to feel like a real half-day ride once stops are included. It mixes waterfront sections with the central and eastern cycling network, so it is especially useful for riders who want variety rather than one long straight trail.

**Best for:** half-day exploration and riders comfortable covering 30–40 km.

### 7. Middle Arm / Shell Road / West Dyke — 43.5 km

**City estimate:** about 2 hours 30 minutes.

This route links three different Richmond riding experiences: the Middle Arm waterfront, the Shell Road corridor and the open West Dyke landscape. It is long enough that wind, food, water and daylight deserve some planning.

**Best for:** stronger recreational riders and visitors who want a long Richmond ride without doing the full circumnavigation.

### 8. Circumnavigate the Island — 47.5 km

**City estimate:** about 3 hours.

This is the big Richmond challenge. The terrain may be flat, but almost 50 km still adds up. Treat it as a half-day adventure rather than a quick spin: allow time for navigation, changing surfaces, riverfront stops and wind. A rider who rarely cycles long distances should start with one of the shorter loops first.

**Best for:** experienced recreational riders, distance goals and a full Richmond cycling day.

## Important current route note: North Dyke closure

The City of Richmond currently shows a North Dyke Trail closure between roughly 1 km east of No. 5 Road and No. 6 Road. The posted detour uses **No. 5 Road → Vulcan Way → No. 6 Road**. Conditions can change, so check the City of Richmond trail map before relying on this detour.

This matters especially when writing or following long loops that use the north side of Richmond. An old map, GPX file or third-party app may still draw a continuous line through a section that is not currently open.

## West Dyke: Richmond's signature scenic ride

If you only have time for one classic Richmond cycling experience, West Dyke is a strong choice. It is flat, visually open and easy to combine with Terra Nova to the north or Steveston to the south. Because the dyke is exposed, however, wind can make an otherwise easy ride feel much harder in one direction.

For photography, late-day light can be excellent, but plan the return before dark. For families, choose a shorter out-and-back instead of assuming everyone will enjoy a full loop. On busy days, ride predictably and give people walking plenty of room.

## South Dyke and Steveston

South Dyke is the natural cycling partner to Steveston. A short ride can become a food-and-waterfront itinerary without much planning: ride first, then stop in the village, or use Steveston as the midpoint of a longer West Dyke / South Dyke loop.

The village itself can be busy with pedestrians and vehicles. Slow down as you approach commercial and waterfront areas, and do not treat a crowded shared space like an uninterrupted bike lane.

## Middle Arm and central Richmond

Middle Arm is useful because it brings cycling closer to central Richmond and the Canada Line. It is a practical choice for visitors who do not have a car, want to shorten a ride with transit, or want a riverfront ride without starting in Steveston.

This is also a good area for building one-way or transit-assisted itineraries. Check current TransLink bicycle rules and station access before the trip, especially during busy travel periods.

## Sea Island

Sea Island is more than the airport. Cycling routes around the island can be combined with Middle Arm for a distinctive 25 km outing. Because roads and transportation facilities around an airport can be confusing, use the current official cycling map rather than improvising from a driving route.

YVR also publishes cycling information for Sea Island. When Wander Bike creates a dedicated Sea Island page, use both Richmond and airport sources so directions stay accurate.

## East Richmond and Queen's Canal

East Richmond offers a quieter contrast to the west-side dyke rides. It is a good choice for local riders who already know Steveston or visitors interested in the agricultural and river landscape. The Queen's Canal and east-side connections can also help build longer loops rather than simple out-and-back rides.

## Can you bike through the George Massey Tunnel?

No. Cyclists cannot simply ride through the Highway 99 / George Massey Tunnel. If your trip needs to cross between Richmond and Delta at this point, plan around transit or the provincial bicycle shuttle when it is operating. Do not route visitors into Highway 99 as if it were a normal cycling connection.

## What kind of bike works best in Richmond?

A comfortable hybrid, city bike or gravel-capable rental is ideal for most recreational Richmond rides. You do not need aggressive mountain-bike suspension for the city's typical dyke and greenway riding, but wider tires can feel more comfortable on unpaved or mixed-surface sections than very narrow road tires.

For long loops, fit matters more than speed. A bike that feels fine for 15 minutes can become uncomfortable after two hours, so adjust saddle height before leaving and carry water.

## Richmond cycling etiquette and safety

Richmond's recreational routes include shared paths. People walking, children, dogs and slower riders may all use the same space. Yield when required, slow down before passing, use a bell or voice early rather than at the last second, and leave enough room to pass safely.

The City of Richmond also publishes local rules and etiquette for shared pathways. Provincial bicycle rules still apply on roads, including helmet, lighting and traffic-control requirements.

## Best time of year to bike Richmond

Richmond can be ridden through much of the year, but spring through early fall usually provides the easiest conditions for visitors. Summer has long daylight and generally dry surfaces, while spring and fall can be quieter. Winter riding is possible on suitable days, but rain, wind, darkness and wet surfaces become bigger planning factors.

Because Richmond is flat and exposed, **wind** deserves more attention than elevation. Check the forecast before committing to a 40–50 km loop, especially with children or inexperienced riders.

## A simple first-time Richmond itinerary

For a first visit, start in or near Steveston, ride a manageable section of South Dyke and West Dyke, stop for food or sightseeing, then return before everyone gets tired. This creates the Richmond experience without forcing a full 26.5 km loop.

For a second visit, try Middle Arm and Sea Island, or move east and explore a different side of the city. Stronger riders can then work up to the 43.5 km route or full 47.5 km circumnavigation.

## More Richmond guides Wander Bike should publish

1. **West Dyke Trail Cycling Guide**
2. **Steveston to South Dyke Bike Ride**
3. **How to Cycle Around Richmond Island — 47.5 km**
4. **Sea Island & Middle Arm Cycling Guide**

6. **Richmond Bike Routes Near the Canada Line**

### Cycling in 100 Mile House, BC

100 Mile House offers useful cycling possibilities for riders interested in Cariboo backcountry, 99 Mile trails and gravel exploration, especially when the trip is planned around the region's established route network. In the wider Cariboo area, the riding character is shaped by big mountain-bike networks, gravel roads and Gold Rush country. Distances are large and services can be sparse, but several Cariboo communities have serious purpose-built mountain-bike networks.

For a first visit, build the day around one of the route ideas below rather than simply choosing the shortest road between two points. Match the route to your bike, surface preference, traffic comfort and available daylight.

100 Mile House is best for riders who enjoy self-directed exploration rather than dense urban cycling infrastructure. Distances between services can grow quickly, so guides should include water, navigation and repair planning.

#### Best bike rides and trail ideas in 100 Mile House

- **99 Mile trail area** — a key mountain-bike/recreation anchor.
- **108 Mile / surrounding backroads** — gravel and mixed-surface day rides.
- **Centennial Park / community riding** — a short local option before longer outings.

#### How to plan the ride

If you are visiting 100 Mile House for the first time, start with the most separated, signed or trail-based option that fits your bike, then add road mileage only after checking the current local map. Weather, construction, wildfire conditions, seasonal maintenance and trail closures can change quickly in B.C., so confirm the latest official notice on the day you ride.

#### Before you ride

- Carry repair gear and offline maps.
- Check wildfire, road and wildlife conditions before remote rides.

B.C. cycling rules still apply: obey traffic controls, ride in the permitted direction, use required lights and reflectors after dark, and wear an approved bicycle helmet. On shared paths, slow down around people walking, dogs, children and other riders; local bylaws or park rules may add extra restrictions.

## Abbotsford

**Classification:** City  
**Region:** Fraser Valley

### Cycling in Abbotsford, BC

Abbotsford is a strong B.C. cycling destination for riders interested in Discovery Trail, dike riding and Fraser Valley farm-country cycling. In the wider Fraser Valley area, the riding character is shaped by river dikes, rail trails, greenways and agricultural-road riding. The valley is often flatter near the Fraser River, while trail systems climb quickly toward the surrounding mountains.

For a first visit, build the day around one of the route ideas below rather than simply choosing the shortest road between two points. Match the route to your bike, surface preference, traffic comfort and available daylight.

Abbotsford is a good example of why a cycling guide should separate relaxed trail riding from rural-road touring. The valley floor can feel easy and open, but road shoulders and farm traffic vary. Build family rides around greenways and dykes; save longer country-road loops for riders who are comfortable sharing the road.

#### Best bike rides and trail ideas in Abbotsford

- **Discovery Trail** — a useful greenway-style spine for a relaxed ride through Abbotsford.
- **Matsqui Trail / Fraser River area** — flat riverside and dyke-oriented riding with broad valley views.
- **Sumas-area dykes and rural connectors** — best for riders comfortable with gravel and shared agricultural roads.

#### How to plan the ride

If you are visiting Abbotsford for the first time, start with the most separated, signed or trail-based option that fits your bike, then add road mileage only after checking the current local map. Weather, construction, wildfire conditions, seasonal maintenance and trail closures can change quickly in B.C., so confirm the latest official notice on the day you ride.

#### Before you ride

- Check flood, construction and seasonal trail notices.
- Agricultural roads can carry large vehicles; ride predictably and stay visible.

B.C. cycling rules still apply: obey traffic controls, ride in the permitted direction, use required lights and reflectors after dark, and wear an approved bicycle helmet. On shared paths, slow down around people walking, dogs, children and other riders; local bylaws or park rules may add extra restrictions.

#### Suggested follow-up articles

- `Discovery Trail: A Local Cycling Guide`
- `Matsqui Trail / Fraser River area: A Local Cycling Guide`
- `A First-Time Cyclist's Guide to Abbotsford, BC`

Chilliwack is easy to package by ability: Valley Rail Trail for a short protected outing, Vedder Rotary Loop for a half-day recreational ride, and Vedder Mountain for mountain biking. - **Vedder Mountain** — mountain biking for riders seeking trail terrain.

### Cycling in Kelowna, BC

Kelowna is a strong B.C. cycling destination for riders interested in Myra Canyon, Okanagan Rail Trail and lake-to-wine-country cycling. In the wider Central Okanagan area, the riding character is shaped by rail trails, lake views and rolling wine-country roads. The valley has excellent low-grade rail-trail riding, but connectors and vineyard roads can be steep and exposed to summer heat.

For a first visit, build the day around one of the route ideas below rather than simply choosing the shortest road between two points. Match the route to your bike, surface preference, traffic comfort and available daylight.

Kelowna is another destination that should have multiple Wander Bike pages. Myra Canyon is the iconic scenic ride; the Okanagan Rail Trail is better for a low-grade corridor ride; and urban waterfront cycling serves casual visitors. As of 2026, do not promise an uninterrupted Kelowna-to-Coldstream ride without checking the construction/gap notice.

#### Best bike rides and trail ideas in Kelowna

- **Myra Canyon — 12 km one way / 24 km round trip**, with 18 trestles and two tunnels.
- **Okanagan Rail Trail** — use the open Kelowna segments and check the airport/Lake Country gap before planning through travel.
- **Waterfront and urban bike connections** — useful for a lighter city day.

#### How to plan the ride

If you are visiting Kelowna for the first time, start with the most separated, signed or trail-based option that fits your bike, then add road mileage only after checking the current local map. In the wider Fraser-Fort George area, the riding character is shaped by northern mountain biking, forest roads and big-distance adventure. Prince George has a developed trail scene; smaller communities lean toward forest roads and locally maintained networks.

If you are visiting McBride for the first time, start with the most separated, signed or trail-based option that fits your bike, then add road mileage only after checking the current local map. ## Richmond

> **Use the full Richmond flagship copy earlier in this file as the primary page.** The compact guide below is retained so the alphabetical database remains complete.

### Cycling in Richmond, BC

Richmond is a strong B.C. cycling destination for riders interested in flat dykes, waterfront greenways and the most visitor-friendly cycling in Metro Vancouver. In the wider Metro Vancouver area, the riding character is shaped by urban bikeways, greenways and regional connections. Conditions vary from flat river lowlands to steep North Shore hills, so the official cycling map matters more than straight-line distance.

For a first visit, build the day around one of the route ideas below rather than simply choosing the shortest road between two points. Match the route to your bike, surface preference, traffic comfort and available daylight.

Richmond is the natural Wander Bike flagship: flat terrain, Steveston, the Fraser River, dykes, transit access and eight city-published scenic loops create enough material for a full guide hub. The North Dyke Trail currently has a closure between roughly 1 km east of No. 5 Road and No. 6 Road; use the posted detour via No. 5 Road, Vulcan Way and No. 6 Road. Riders cannot cycle through the Highway 99 / George Massey Tunnel and must use transit or the provincial bike shuttle when operating.

#### Best bike rides and trail ideas in Richmond

- **Steveston / South Dyke — 6 km / about 20 min** (City estimate at 18 km/h).
- **West Dyke / South Dyke — 26.5 km / about 90 min**.
- **East Richmond South / North Arm River — 23.5 km / about 75 min**.
- **Sea Island Drive / Middle Arm — 25 km / about 85 min**.
- **West and South Dyke / Sea Island — 30 km / about 1 h 45 min**.
- **Middle Arm / East & Central Richmond — 38 km / about 2 h**.
- **Middle Arm / Shell Road / West Dyke — 43.5 km / about 2 h 30 min**.
- **Circumnavigate the Island — 47.5 km / about 3 h**.

#### How to plan the ride

If you are visiting Richmond for the first time, start with the most separated, signed or trail-based option that fits your bike, then add road mileage only after checking the current local map. Weather, construction, wildfire conditions, seasonal maintenance and trail closures can change quickly in B.C., so confirm the latest official notice on the day you ride.

#### Before you ride

- Check TransLink's current local cycling map before choosing a route.
- Use transit to shorten one-way rides or bypass uncomfortable road sections where permitted.

B.C. cycling rules still apply: obey traffic controls, ride in the permitted direction, use required lights and reflectors after dark, and wear an approved bicycle helmet. On shared paths, slow down around people walking, dogs, children and other riders; local bylaws or park rules may add extra restrictions.

#### Suggested follow-up articles

- `Steveston / South Dyke: A Local Cycling Guide`
- `West Dyke / South Dyke: A Local Cycling Guide`

In the wider Kootenay Boundary area, the riding character is shaped by historic rail corridors and one of B.C.'s strongest mountain-bike clusters. The C&W/KVR corridor provides long-distance options, but surfaces vary. Rossland-area singletrack is much more technical.

For a first visit, build the day around one of the route ideas below rather than simply choosing the shortest road between two points. Match the route to your bike, surface preference, traffic comfort and available daylight.

Rossland is an expert-content opportunity. Do not flatten it into a generic 'best bike rides' article: publish a beginner/local-network guide, a Seven Summits planning guide and a RED Bike Park guide separately.

- **RED Mountain Bike Park** — lift-accessed riding operating in the 2026 summer season.

#### How to plan the ride

If you are visiting Rossland for the first time, start with the most separated, signed or trail-based option that fits your bike, then add road mileage only after checking the current local map. Weather, construction, wildfire conditions, seasonal maintenance and trail closures can change quickly in B.C., so confirm the latest official notice on the day you ride.

#### Before you ride

- Do not assume a rail trail is smooth: ballast, ruts, sand and washouts occur.
- Check seasonal opening dates for alpine and lift-served riding.

B.C. cycling rules still apply: obey traffic controls, ride in the permitted direction, use required lights and reflectors after dark, and wear an approved bicycle helmet. Revelstoke and Golden are mountain-bike destinations; the Shuswap corridor is gaining accessible rail-trail mileage but remains incomplete in places.

For a first visit, build the day around one of the route ideas below rather than simply choosing the shortest road between two points. Match the route to your bike, surface preference, traffic comfort and available daylight.

As of July 3, 2026, approximately 32 km of the overall rail trail was open across three disconnected segments; Sicamous-to-Grindrod is the longest continuous open piece. A temporary September closure was planned for a section adjacent to Mara Lake for shoreline work, so this page needs a live condition check before publication and again before peak fall travel.

#### Best bike rides and trail ideas in Sicamous

- **Sicamous to Grindrod — km 0.5–27.3 open** in the July 2026 project update.
- **Short Mara Lake out-and-back** — choose a turnaround within the officially open segment.
- **Downtown/start-point spin** — useful for families who do not want the full open distance.

#### How to plan the ride

If you are visiting Sicamous for the first time, start with the most separated, signed or trail-based option that fits your bike, then add road mileage only after checking the current local map. Weather, construction, wildfire conditions, seasonal maintenance and trail closures can change quickly in B.C., so confirm the latest official notice on the day you ride.

#### Before you ride

- Check current rail-trail segment openings.
- Mountain routes can remain snow-covered well after valley trails are dry.

B.C. cycling rules still apply: obey traffic controls, ride in the permitted direction, use required lights and reflectors after dark, and wear an approved bicycle helmet. On shared paths, slow down around people walking, dogs, children and other riders; local bylaws or park rules may add extra restrictions.

#### Suggested follow-up articles

- `Sicamous to Grindrod: A Local Cycling Guide`
- `Short Mara Lake out-and-back: A Local Cycling Guide`
- `A First-Time Cyclist's Guide to Sicamous, BC`

### Cycling in Smithers, BC

Smithers is a strong B.C. cycling destination for riders interested in Cycle 16, gravel loops and Hudson Bay Mountain riding. In the wider Bulkley-Nechako area, the riding character is shaped by Bulkley Valley gravel, mountain biking and northern lake-country riding. Smithers and Burns Lake are established riding centres; smaller communities offer quieter but less formally mapped riding.

For a first visit, build the day around one of the route ideas below rather than simply choosing the shortest road between two points. Match the route to your bike, surface preference, traffic comfort and available daylight.

Smithers stands out because it supports three separate cycling products: accessible paved path, long gravel/road loops and technical mountain biking. The guide should explain that the eventual Smithers–Telkwa Cycle 16 connection is not yet fully complete.

#### Best bike rides and trail ideas in Smithers

- **Cycle 16 Phase 1 — about 7 km each way** from downtown toward Highway 16/New Babine Lake Road.
- **Tyhee Lake / Telkwa-area loops** — scenic rural-road and gravel options.
- **Tour de Smithers routes — 42 km and 85 km** examples for stronger riders.
- **Hudson Bay Mountain / Bluff-area trails** — mountain biking for a range of abilities.

#### How to plan the ride

If you are visiting Smithers for the first time, start with the most separated, signed or trail-based option that fits your bike, then add road mileage only after checking the current local map. Weather, construction, wildfire conditions, seasonal maintenance and trail closures can change quickly in B.C., so confirm the latest official notice on the day you ride.

#### Before you ride

- Use local trail-association maps for mountain-bike networks.
- Expect limited cell service outside town and carry a repair kit.

B.C. cycling rules still apply: obey traffic controls, ride in the permitted direction, use required lights and reflectors after dark, and wear an approved bicycle helmet. On shared paths, slow down around people walking, dogs, children and other riders; local bylaws or park rules may add extra restrictions.

### Cycling in Summerland, BC

Summerland offers useful cycling possibilities for riders interested in KVR history, orchards and dry-interior gravel riding, especially when the trip is planned around the region's established route network. In the wider Okanagan-Similkameen area, the riding character is shaped by Kettle Valley rail-trail riding, vineyards and dry-interior scenery. Gentle historic rail grades are the signature experience, but some KVR sections are rough, motorized or temporarily closed.

For a first visit, build the day around one of the route ideas below rather than simply choosing the shortest road between two points. Match the route to your bike, surface preference, traffic comfort and available daylight.

#### Best bike rides and trail ideas in Summerland

- **KVR near Summerland/Faulder** — historic rail corridor with mixed-use/motorized sections in places.
- **Orchard and rural-road loops** — scenic but hilly and shared with vehicles.

#### How to plan the ride

If you are visiting Summerland for the first time, start with the most separated, signed or trail-based option that fits your bike, then add road mileage only after checking the current local map. Weather, construction, wildfire conditions, seasonal maintenance and trail closures can change quickly in B.C., so confirm the latest official notice on the day you ride.

#### Before you ride

- Check the latest KVR closure map immediately before riding.
- Use gravel or wider tires for remote or mixed-use KVR sections.

B.C. cycling rules still apply: obey traffic controls, ride in the permitted direction, use required lights and reflectors after dark, and wear an approved bicycle helmet. On shared paths, slow down around people walking, dogs, children and other riders; local bylaws or park rules may add extra restrictions.

## Tofino

**Classification:** District municipality  
**Region:** Alberni-Clayoquot  
**Suggested URL:** `/guides/tofino-bc-cycling-guide`  
**SEO title:** `Cycling in Tofino, BC: Best Bike Rides & Trails | Wander Bike`  
**Meta description:** `Bike Tofino, BC: route ideas, trail highlights, terrain notes and practical planning tips for a better local ride.`

### Cycling in Tofino, BC

Tofino is a strong B.C. cycling destination for riders interested in a car-light Pacific coast ride linking town, beaches and Pacific Rim National Park Reserve. In the wider Alberni-Clayoquot area, the riding character is shaped by Pacific coast pathways, valley roads and all-weather adventure. The west coast is wet, exposed and busy in peak season. Purpose-built multi-use paths are the best choice for casual riders; backroads suit more experienced riders.

For a first visit, build the day around one of the route ideas below rather than simply choosing the shortest road between two points. Match the route to your bike, surface preference, traffic comfort and available daylight.

This is one of the cleanest visitor-oriented cycling experiences in B.C.: riders can leave town on a purpose-built path and continue into the national park. Parks Canada says cycling in the Long Beach Unit is permitted on beaches and ʔapsčiik t̓ašii, not on most hiking trails. Winter debris and weather are significant; the pathway is best enjoyed mid-April to mid-October.

#### Best bike rides and trail ideas in Tofino

- **Tofino Multi-Use Path — 8.8 km**, connecting town with neighbourhoods and beaches.
- **ʔapsčiik t̓ašii — approximately 25 km**, paved through the Long Beach Unit of Pacific Rim National Park Reserve.
- **Combined Tofino + national-park out-and-back** — scale the distance to weather and rider ability.

#### How to plan the ride

If you are visiting Tofino for the first time, start with the most separated, signed or trail-based option that fits your bike, then add road mileage only after checking the current local map. Weather, construction, wildfire conditions, seasonal maintenance and trail closures can change quickly in B.C., so confirm the latest official notice on the day you ride.

#### Before you ride

- Check Parks Canada restrictions before entering national-park trails.
- Use fenders/lights in wet weather and expect wildlife and debris after storms.

B.C. cycling rules still apply: obey traffic controls, ride in the permitted direction, use required lights and reflectors after dark, and wear an approved bicycle helmet. On shared paths, slow down around people walking, dogs, children and other riders; local bylaws or park rules may add extra restrictions.

#### Suggested follow-up articles

- `Tofino Multi-Use Path: A Local Cycling Guide`
- `ʔapsčiik t̓ašii: A Local Cycling Guide`
- `A First-Time Cyclist's Guide to Tofino, BC`

### Cycling in West Kelowna, BC

West Kelowna offers useful cycling possibilities for riders interested in Okanagan lake views and steep wine-country road riding, especially when the trip is planned around the region's established route network. In the wider Central Okanagan area, the riding character is shaped by rail trails, lake views and rolling wine-country roads. The valley has excellent low-grade rail-trail riding, but connectors and vineyard roads can be steep and exposed to summer heat.

For a first visit, build the day around one of the route ideas below rather than simply choosing the shortest road between two points. Match the route to your bike, surface preference, traffic comfort and available daylight.

#### Best bike rides and trail ideas in West Kelowna

- **West Kelowna community/waterfront connections** — choose lower-stress local routes.
- **Wine-country road loop** — best for experienced cyclists or e-bike riders after checking grades/traffic.

#### How to plan the ride

If you are visiting West Kelowna for the first time, start with the most separated, signed or trail-based option that fits your bike, then add road mileage only after checking the current local map. Weather, construction, wildfire conditions, seasonal maintenance and trail closures can change quickly in B.C., so confirm the latest official notice on the day you ride.

#### Before you ride

- Check current Okanagan Rail Trail gap/construction notices before planning a through-ride.
- Carry more water than you think you need during hot Okanagan afternoons.

B.C. cycling rules still apply: obey traffic controls, ride in the permitted direction, use required lights and reflectors after dark, and wear an approved bicycle helmet. On shared paths, slow down around people walking, dogs, children and other riders; local bylaws or park rules may add extra restrictions.

Squamish, Whistler and Pemberton each have distinct riding identities.

If you are visiting Whistler for the first time, start with the most separated, signed or trail-based option that fits your bike, then add road mileage only after checking the current local map. # Editorial checklist before each page goes live

- Reopen every source listed in that destination's hidden editorial note.
- Confirm the route is currently open and bicycle access is legal.
- Confirm whether the surface is paved, gravel, natural surface or mixed.
- Check for construction, wildfire, washout, seasonal gate or ferry/transit changes.
- Check e-bike rules separately where the route is in a park, protected area or locally managed trail.
- Do not call a walking/hiking trail a bike trail unless the manager explicitly allows bicycles.
- For Indigenous lands, protected cultural areas or Nation-managed access, use Nation-led sources and preferred place names.
- If using an approximate time, say it is an estimate and explain the assumed pace when practical.
- Add `Last checked: YYYY-MM-DD` to high-risk route-status pages.
- Use original Wander Bike photography where possible. If you have not personally ridden the route, avoid first-person claims such as “we rode this yesterday.”
